                               Refer San Francisco letter dated September 4, 1947.
                                                                             Portland, reported
               without                                                     prospe~ting at a
               point in the Cascade l:ountains approJdmately five thousand feet from sea level,
               during the afternoon he noticed a reflection, looked up, and sa\V a disc pro-
               ceeding in a southeasterly direction, Immediately upon sighting this object
               he placed his telescope to his eye and observed the disc for approximately
               forty-five to sixty second•· He remarked that it is possible for him to pick
                                                                                                                                                                                                               b1L
               up an object at a distance or ten miles with his telescope. At the time the.
               disc was sighted by J.lr. , - . i t was banking in the sun, and he observed
               five or six similar objects but only concentrated on one. He ~lated that
               they did not fly in any particular formation and that he would estimate their
               height to be about one thousand feet from where he was standing. He said the
               object was about thirty feet in diameter.and appeared to have a tail. lt made
               no noise.
                          According to         he remained in the vicinity of the Cascades :
               for several days and then returned to Portland and noted an article in the       /
               local paper which stated in effect that a man in Boise, Idaho, had sighted a j)
               sil'lilar object but that authorities had disclaimed any knowledge of such an
                                                                                                    ~                                                                                                           1
               ocject. He said he communicated with the Army for the sole purpose of attempt-
               ing to add credence to the story furnished by the man in Boise.
                              '
                   ,      lllllil-a1so related that on the occasion or his sighting the objects
               on lune 24, 1947 he had in his possession a combination compass and watch. He I
               noted particularly that immediately before he sighted the disc the compass         t)~~
               acted very peculiar, the hand waving from one side to the dther, but that this
               condition corrected itself immediately after the discs had passed out of sight.
                    Informant appeared to be a very reliable individual who advised that
           he had been a prospector in the states of .Montana, Ylashington, and Oregon for
           the past forty years,

                                                                                J~ 7, 1947:

                        A storm had occurred in Hloenix on the afternoon of July 7, 1947,
            and the clouds were at approximately 5000 feet, the color of which was grey
            and they resembled dense cumulus. A f.ifteen JDile per hour wind was blowing.
            At approximately 5 p.m. on that date, Mr.           as walking from his home
            to the              and he heard a noise resembling a P-80 aircraft, apparently
            coming in a west.erly direction. Upon searching the sld.es he observed to the
            northeast what appeared to be an odd shaped ship. He described this ship as
            being possibly 20 to .30 feet in diameter, traveling at an estinated speed of
            100 JDiles. per hour. It appeared to be mald.ng a spiral in a small radius of    fI
            possibly one-half to three quarters of a mile.            tated the sound which lJ t'C.
. .-,:-!    he first heard was no longer audible; that he hurriedly procured a 120 Brownie
            Box Camera from his shop, and as the aircraft completed the first circle he
            obtained a photograph. As the ship started on the second turn, it was in a
            northwesterly direction from his place and he again snapped a photograph, which
            more closely resembled the shape of the ship than photograph #l, this being
            designated as Exhibit II, as submitted to the Bureau with letter dated August
            4, 1947, by the Office of Assistant Chief of Staff A-2 Intelligence, Hamilton
            Field, California. ._further described the di-rection of the ship by";
            stating that instead of completing the lefthand downward spiral the ship banked
            to the right and disappeared into the clouds. He stated that just as it banked,
            he had snapped the second photograph, Exhibit II.

           •·    At the last point, which was at the time the ship made this turn, »r •
           .-est~ted it to be between 1,000 and 2,000 feet high. He further des- L 'I~
            cribed the appearance of this ship as closely resembling a picture which appear& /',..
            on the front cover of the :May, 194?, issue of the Mechanics Illustrated l&lgazine,
            ~e only dif:rerence noted by l!r.        being t.hat the ship appeared t.o be fly-
            ing backward.

                       Mr • . _ . a l s o related that he did not associate the appearance of
            t_bis ship with the numerous reports of flying discs. He related, however, that
            after a three JDinute interview with newspaper reporters the~ that appeared         ·
            in the local papers was "blown up" to the extent that he,~ barely recog-
            nized the information furnished by him. )lr.-.explained that the afore-
            mentioned di.mensions and distances 11ere only estimated, pointing out, that his l "1/'
            previous experience probabl,y assisted him in making fairly close estimates. HeiJ fV
            related that he had been doing ex:perimentalwork for the past ten years. He
            also stated he was EIDPloyed at the Naval Ordnance Laboratory, Washington, D. c.
            during Uul early IBrt of World War II and left there the beginning of 1942.
            Upon his return to Phoenix, he was employed at Falcon Field near Phoenix which
            was leased by the British Government for training purposes. He instructed in
            instrument training, aircraft identification and gunner; practice. He explained
             that his hobbies have .to do with science, physics, radio, electronics and astronomy.
           ·He produced a model which he had made of a radio controlled airplane. He related
            that most of his work consists of building transformers, but it is also necessary
            for him to depend upon his music abiliiQr for his livlihood.
                                                  -2-
     •                                                         ,..) ~­


         Phoenix file 62-213
                   .....

                In explaining the results of photographing the ship, Mr . . . . . .
      stated that he was surprised that the object appeared dark on a light back-
      ground; that he fully expected that the object would be light on a dark back-
      ground. He described the light spot which appears in the center of tile object
      (Exhibit ll) as being a green house.          insisted that the ship made no
      noise that was audible to his ear and he was unable to detect the presence of 0
                                                                                        /1C,
      aey propeller. He also related that after the ne.vspaper release over Radio
      Station KTAR, wherein statements were made that he,~ had stated Army
      officials were studying tile photographs, and that it was a top secret, he
      attempted to determine the source of this release and the Radio station finally
      told him that it was a United Press release froo Washington, D. C.

         and interrogation:             ·

                           Arary and that he would not get them back.

                                      The following investigation was conducted at Canyonville and
                             uyrtle Creek, Oregon on August 12 1 1947.

                                                                                Roseburg, Oregon, operator
                                                                    , Oregon, advised that on 8-6-47 at
                                              :15 PM while instructing a student in a take off at the airport,
                              he noticed· an object east of Myrtle Creek which appeared to be from 5 1 000 to
                              8,000 feet in the air. The sky was completely clear and visibility was
                              excellent. He stated that the object glistened and appeared to be of aluminium
                              sheeting. Upon noticing it he had immediately taken over the controls of the
.- ....   ....
          ·
                              plane which at that time was at 400 feet, and proceeded East in an attempt to
          ·-:-                further observe the object. He advised that the object appeared to be           /_
                              climbing and traveling East at a high rate of speed which-he estimated on a /.1 7~
                              computer in his plane as 1 1000 miles per hour. He believed the object to be       ·
                              spherical in shape and recalled noticing a darker object to the right the first
                              time he saw it. He observed no vapor trails nor did he hear any noise fro~ the
 ;b~.·                        object.  Aft~r  s arching the area for approximately 10 minutes.-..and
  -~~         .'              his student                returned to the airport and n~de another landing and
.,d~)                         take off. In practical y the same position at 400 feet altitude, both ..........
                             -and           saw the object in approximately the same position as seen before.
 -_
. •-          .
              !·
              ;               He estimated the sphere to be 30 feet in diameter and stated when they first
                              observed i~ it appeared to be so near he could fly right to it. However, the
                              object sighted the second time disappeared in the same manner as the first had •
                                .I
                                      It is noted that
                             Air Corps for about       3t

                      that Los Angeles papers carried under a r.ashington date line an article
